orientation on the right. This includes gauge-side rank drop and genus
one. Evaluation at infinity and quotienting by $G_\RR$ are separate
finite-dimensional operations.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We first compute the holomorphic deformation groups. The primitive
extension has the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:primitive-global-extension}
 0\longrightarrow\cO_{\PP^1}(1)\boxtimes L
 \longrightarrow E\longrightarrow
 \cO_{\PP^1}\boxtimes(\Lambda L^{-1})\longrightarrow0;
\end{equation}
see \cite[Proposition~2]{Munoz99b} for $g\geq2$. The same extension
construction applies when $g=1$, and the following calculation treats
that case directly. On each horizontal line
$C_x=\PP^1\times\{x\}$ the extension splits, since
$H^1(\PP^1,\cO(1))=0$. Hence
\[
 (\End_0E\otimes K_X)|_{C_x}
 \cong\cO(-1)\oplus\cO(-2)\oplus\cO(-3).
\]
It has no section, so Serre duality gives $H^2(X,\End_0E)=0$.
A trace-free holomorphic endomorphism vanishes on the open set of stable
surface fibers containing the seam, hence $H^0(X,\End_0E)=0$.
Riemann--Roch gives
\[
 \chi(\End_0E)=3(1-g)-2=1-3g,\qquad h^1(X,\End_0E)=3g-1.
\]
Vanishing of $H^2$ makes the fixed-determinant deformation problem
unobstructed. Equivalently, the integrability equation in a Dolbeault
slice has surjective linearization onto $(0,2)$-forms, and the
implicit-function theorem integrates every $H^1$ class.

Now take a mixed kernel element $(a,\nu)$. DFL regularity makes it
smooth. The integrability row of the ASD linearization gives a Dolbeault
cocycle $v_-=a^{0,1}$ on the negative half. The positive equation makes
$\nu$ a holomorphic infinitesimal map; pulling back the universal bundle
over the dual numbers gives its Dolbeault cocycle $v_+$. Identify the
original holomorphic bundles on both halves with the restrictions of
$E$. Matching says that their vertical cohomology classes agree on the
seam. Since the seam fibers are simple, there is a unique smooth
trace-free $\xi$ there such that
\[
 (v_--v_+)_\Sigma=\bar\partial_\alpha\xi.
\]
Extend $\xi$ smoothly to the negative side and subtract
$\bar\partial_E\widetilde\xi$ from $v_-$. The resulting piecewise
$(0,1)$-form has equal vertical traces and is distributionally
$\bar\partial_E$-closed. Indeed, the possible seam delta term is
$d\bar z$ wedged with the jump in the vertical component. A jump in the
coefficient of $d\bar z$ contributes zero because
$d\bar z\wedge d\bar z=0$. The $L^2$ Dolbeault Hodge decomposition on
the closed surface therefore gives a class in $H^1(X,\End_0E)$.
Different extensions give global $W^{1,2}$ zero-forms whose Dolbeault
derivatives are the differences. Changing cocycle representatives has
the same effect. This defines \eqref{eq:dolbeault-tangent-obstruction}
without an extension on the complete mixed Sobolev domain.

Suppose the class is zero. Write the corrected piecewise cocycle as
$\bar\partial_E\epsilon$ with a global $W^{1,2}$ zero-form $\epsilon$.
Its vertical class on the positive side is zero, so $\nu=0$. Choose
$v_+=0$. Then $\epsilon$ is holomorphic on the positive side and
vanishes there by simplicity of its stable surface fibers. Its seam
trace is zero. On the negative side,
\[
 a^{0,1}=\bar\partial_E\eta,\qquad
 \eta=\epsilon+\widetilde\xi,\qquad\eta|_\Gamma=\xi.
\]
The real matching condition now says $a_\Sigma=d_\alpha\xi$ with
$\xi$ skew-Hermitian. Uniqueness of its vertical Dolbeault primitive
identifies it with the same $\xi$. Dirichlet elliptic regularity makes
$\eta$ smooth. Decompose $\eta=\chi+s$ into skew-Hermitian and
self-adjoint parts; then $s|_\Gamma=0$. Removing the infinitesimal
unitary gauge direction $\chi$ leaves a Hermitian-metric variation of
the fixed holomorphic bundle. The linearized HYM equation is its
Dirichlet Chern Laplacian, so integration by parts gives $s=0$.
Thus $a=d_A\chi$. The Coulomb row and normal boundary condition give
\[
 d_A^*d_A\chi=0,\qquad \nabla_{\mathbf n}\chi=0.
\]
A second integration by parts gives $d_A\chi=0$, and hence $a=0$.
This proves injectivity, including the unitary gauge direction.

Conversely, integrate an $H^1$ class to a family of holomorphic bundles.
Seam admissibility persists for sufficiently small parameters by
openness of stability over the compact positive hemisphere. Reconstruct
by Theorem~\ref{thm:reconstruction} at the fixed scale and differentiate.
Impose Coulomb gauge and zero normal component by solving the covariant
Neumann equation for an infinitesimal unitary gauge transformation.
Its kernel consists of parallel trace-free endomorphisms and vanishes
by irreducibility of a seam fiber. This gauge change preserves the
linearized equations and matching, and doubling the resulting variation
returns the prescribed $H^1$ class. The kernel identification is onto.

Finally, Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-mixed-index} and $p_1(\ad E)=-2$ give
real index $6g-2$, equal to the kernel dimension. The cokernel is zero,
and the complex orientation of the kernel orients the determinant line.
\end{proof}

On compact families with scales bounded away from zero, the kernel
bundle has locally bounded complementary right inverses. Uniform
estimates as $\rho\to0$ require the original-coordinate construction
in Section~\ref{sec:regular-local-gluing}. The comparison with DFL's
coherent orientations is established in
Sections~\ref{sec:gluing-orientations} and~\ref{sec:reference-comparison}.

The rank-drop normal can be read explicitly.  At a tensor
$[v\otimes a]\in\PP(V\otimes\CC^2)$, let
$\ell_v=\CC v$ and $\ell_a=\CC a$.  The normal to the Segre locus is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Segre-normal}
 \Hom(\ell_v,V/\ell_v)\otimes
 \Hom(\ell_a,\CC^2/\ell_a),
\end{equation}
of complex dimension $g-1$.  In genus two it is a complex line; after its
basepoint is fixed, both real components remain as smoothing directions
from rank drop to a full-rank bubble.  The seam-preserving affine group has
no rotational factor and does not remove the phase.  Thus the genus-two
Segre locus has real codimension two inside the primitive face.  In genus
one the normal has rank zero, leaving the elliptic excess family.

\subsection{Primitive no-neck at good circles}\label{sec:primitive-no-neck}
Let $(A_\nu,u_\nu)$ be a sequence with one primitive matching center
$z_\nu\to z_0$ and concentration scales $\rho_\nu\to0$, and suppose its
doubled bundles have a limit $E$ in the three-colored master
compactification described after Proposition~\ref{prop:framed-splitting}.
Write
$\cE_\nu(a,b)$ for the normalized mixed energy in the paired half-annulus
$a<|z-z_\nu|<b$.

\begin{proposition}[primitive good-circle no-neck]
\label{prop:primitive-no-neck}
After passage to a subsequence, there are radii
\[
                 \rho_\nu\ll a_\nu\ll b_\nu\longrightarrow0
\]
such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:no-neck}
                         \cE_\nu(a_\nu,b_\nu)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
The capped region inside $a_\nu$ has clutching sector one and energy
tending to $\frac12$.  Hence no positive-energy atom or further analytic
scale lies between the primitive core and the residual solution.  On the
two cut circles, the spliced representatives converge in a common seam
gauge to the same flat attaching representative.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The stable limit is regular at infinity.  For a sufficiently large
rescaled radius $R$, cap its tail in a contractible fine-moduli chart.
The finite charge identity in Proposition~\ref{prop:framed-splitting}
shows that, for $R$ and then $\nu$
large, the capped region $|z-z_\nu|\leq R\rho_\nu$ has clutching sector
one.  This includes rank drop because the lost map degree is exactly the
length-one elementary modification.  The local charge theorem gives the
same sector for the outer atom.  Every annulus between the two caps
therefore has sector zero.

Put $E_\nu(r)=\cE_\nu(0,r)$.  First choose the outer cuts from smooth
convergence on compact subsets of the punctured disc to the residual
solution.  DFL removability extends that solution over $z_0$, so a
diagonal choice gives $b_\nu\to0$, with
$b_\nu/\rho_\nu\to\infty$, such that the outer-tail energy relative to
the residual solution and $b_\nu E_\nu'(b_\nu)$ both tend to zero.

Since
\[
 \int_{r_1}^{r_2}rE_\nu'(r)\,d\log r
       =E_\nu(r_2)-E_\nu(r_1),
\]
choose $R_\nu\to\infty$ slowly enough that
$R_\nu^2\rho_\nu<b_\nu$, and subdivide the logarithmic region from
$R_\nu\rho_\nu$ to $R_\nu^2\rho_\nu$ into dyadic shells.  Bounded total
energy and coarea give a shell of energy tending to zero and a radius
$a_\nu$ in it for which
\begin{equation}\label{eq:good-circle}
 a_\nu E_\nu'(a_\nu)+b_\nu E_\nu'(b_\nu)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}

At either good circle form DFL's spliced connection $B_{\nu,r}$ on
$S^1\times\Sigma_g$: it joins the gauge semicircle to a connection
representing the holomorphic semicircle through the flat interpolation
strip.  The small-energy mean-value estimate on the selected shell and
the proof of \cite[Lemma~4.62]{DaeFukLip21b} give the localized slice bound
\[
 |\CS_{\beta_{\nu,r}}(B_{\nu,r})|
             \leq C rE_\nu'(r).
\]
The flat references at the two radii are joined by the seam path, so the
Chern--Simons functional is independent of that choice.  The Stokes
calculation of \cite[Lemma~4.67]{DaeFukLip21b}, now on the annulus, gives
\[
 \cE_\nu(a_\nu,b_\nu)
 =\CS(B_{\nu,b_\nu})-\CS(B_{\nu,a_\nu})
\]
with the normalization of \eqref{eq:top-energy}.  The possible integral
change of lift is zero because the annulus has clutching sector zero.
Equation~\eqref{eq:good-circle} proves \eqref{eq:no-neck}.

The total local energy tends to $\frac12$, the annular energy vanishes,
and the outer-tail energy tends to that of the removable residual
solution.  Thus the sector-one inner core has energy $\frac12$ in the
limit.  A further atom would have positive
half-integral charge, whereas a diffuse remainder is excluded by the
same Chern--Simons identity.  Finally, DFL's small-energy Coulomb gauges
on the two selected shells converge to the flat seam representatives;
stable reduction at infinity identifies both with the attaching value.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Inverse-adiabatic Dirichlet estimates}
\label{sec:uniform-inverse}
Put $\tau=\rho^2$.  Since the K\"ahler form is a product, the trace-free
HYM equation is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tau-HYM}
 i\Lambda_{H_-}F_H^\perp
       +\tau i\Lambda_{\Sigma_g}F_H^\perp=0.
\end{equation}
Thus the limit $\tau=0$ is elliptic in the base variable and regards the
surface variable as a parameter.  This is the useful direction of the
inverse-adiabatic limit.

For the following statements, $W^{r,s;p}_{B,\Sigma}$ controls mixed
derivatives with at most $r$ base derivatives and at most $s$ surface
derivatives in fixed product charts.  On the HYM-equation
component we use the equivalent $\rho$-weighted target norm obtained by
multiplying the equation by $\rho^2$, as in
\eqref{eq:tau-HYM}. The next theorem concerns the fixed compact base;
estimates at the physical attaching end are established in
Section~\ref{sec:regular-local-gluing}.

\begin{theorem}[Dirichlet regularity on a fixed compact product]
\label{thm:inverse-adiabatic}
Let $C\subset\frakM_g^{\rm sa}/G_\RR$ be compact, represented in smooth
local families of bundles and boundary metrics on the fixed compact
hemisphere. The auxiliary Dirichlet metrics $H_{E,\rho}$ extend smoothly
in $(E,\tau)$ to $\tau=0$ in every fixed-coordinate
$W^{r,s;p}_{B,\Sigma}$ space. The limiting metric is the unique
fixed-determinant solution of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:base-flat-limit}
 i\Lambda_{H_-}F_{H_{E,0}}^\perp=0,
 \qquad H_{E,0}|_{\Gamma\times\Sigma_g}=H_\Gamma.
\end{equation}
Moreover, $H_{E,0}^{-1}H_{E,\rho}-1=O(\tau)$ in every fixed-coordinate
$C^k$ norm, uniformly over $C$. Interior rank drop is allowed. This
statement concerns the smooth compact metric \eqref{eq:omega-rho}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $B=H_-$. For each $x\in\Sigma_g$, Donaldson's Dirichlet theorem
on the complex disc gives the solution $H_{E,0}$ of
\eqref{eq:base-flat-limit}. Its linearization is the invertible base
Dirichlet Chern Laplacian. Ordinary parameter-dependent elliptic theory
therefore gives smooth dependence on $x$ and $E$. No surface derivative
is inverted at this step.

We use norms which include pointwise control. Fix $0<\alpha<1$ and use
the metric
\[
                    \widehat g_\rho=g_B+\rho^{-2}g_{\Sigma_g}.
\]
Define $\|\cdot\|_{k,\alpha;\rho}$ as the supremum of local
$C^{k,\alpha}$ norms in product charts of a fixed small base radius and
surface radius proportional to $\rho$, pulling back the surface
coordinate by $x=x_0+\rho y$. Include the fixed base boundary charts.
Thus base derivatives have weight one and surface derivatives have
weight $\rho$. In particular,
\[
 \|s\|_\infty\leq\|s\|_{k,\alpha;\rho},\qquad
 \|st\|_{k,\alpha;\rho}
 \leq C_k\|s\|_{k,\alpha;\rho}\|t\|_{k,\alpha;\rho}.
\]
These estimates follow in each fixed rescaled chart and then by taking
the supremum. Their constants do not depend on the number of charts.

Put
\[
 \Phi_\tau(H)=i\Lambda_BF_H^\perp+
                    \tau i\Lambda_{\Sigma_g}F_H^\perp.
\]
We first establish a linear estimate at any smooth approximate family
$K_\tau$ whose contracted curvature $\Phi_\tau(K_\tau)$ tends uniformly
to zero. Assume that $K_\tau$ and its inverse have uniform bounds in
all fixed-coordinate derivatives. Transport the moment map to the fixed
metric by
\[
 \Psi_{K,\tau}(s)=e^{s/2}\Phi_\tau(K_\tau e^s)e^{-s/2}.
\]
Its real linearization with zero Dirichlet boundary value is
\[
 \begin{split}
 L_{K,\tau}s
 &=i\Lambda_{\widehat g_\rho}\bar\partial\partial_Ks
                  +\tfrac12[s,\Phi_\tau(K_\tau)]\\
 &=\tfrac12\nabla_K^*\nabla_Ks.
 \end{split}
\]
Indeed, the K\"ahler identity for the first term adds
$\tfrac12[\Phi_\tau(K_\tau),s]$ to half the covariant Laplacian;
transport cancels that commutator. This holds also at the approximate
metric. Kato's inequality therefore gives
\[
 (\Delta_B+\tau\Delta_{\Sigma_g})|s|
 \leq C|L_{K,\tau}s|,
\]
where the scalar Laplacians have nonnegative spectrum. If
$\Delta_B\varphi=1$ and $\varphi|_{\partial B}=0$, comparison with
$C\varphi\|L_{K,\tau}s\|_\infty$ gives a uniform $C^0$ estimate.
Interior and Dirichlet boundary Schauder estimates in the rescaled charts
then give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-holder-inverse}
 \|s\|_{k+2,\alpha;\rho}
 \leq C_k\|L_{K,\tau}s\|_{k,\alpha;\rho}.
\end{equation}
The coefficients, scaled coefficient derivatives, and ellipticity
constants are uniform. The global zeroth-order bound uses the fixed base
barrier. For each positive $\tau$ the Dirichlet operator has index zero,
by deformation to its connection Laplacian. The estimate gives
injectivity and hence surjectivity.

Construct formal coefficients before applying contraction. The derivative
of $\Phi_0$ at $H_{E,0}$ is the base Dirichlet Chern Laplacian $L_0^B$.
For each integer $N\geq0$, successive equations for this operator give
smooth sections $s_j$, zero on the boundary, such that
\[
 H_\tau^{[N]}=H_{E,0}\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^N\tau^j s_j\right),
 \qquad
 \Phi_\tau(H_\tau^{[N]})=O(\tau^{N+1})
\]
in every fixed-coordinate $C^k$ norm. At order $j$, the unknown term is
$L_0^Bs_j$; all other terms involve already constructed smooth
coefficients. This avoids an implicit-function theorem at $\tau=0$
with loss of surface derivatives.

Apply \eqref{eq:compact-holder-inverse} at $H_\tau^{[N]}$. In the scaled
H\"older spaces, the exponential-coordinate nonlinear remainder obeys
\[
 \|\mathcal N_\tau(s)-\mathcal N_\tau(t)\|_{0,\alpha;\rho}
 \leq C(\|s\|_{2,\alpha;\rho}+\|t\|_{2,\alpha;\rho})
              \|s-t\|_{2,\alpha;\rho}.
\]
Indeed, in the fixed rescaled charts the principal coefficients are smooth
matrix functions of the metric variable, and the remaining nonlinear
terms are products of at most two first derivatives. The preceding
product estimate makes the constant uniform. Contraction produces
\[
 H_{E,\rho}=H_\tau^{[N]}e^{r_{N,\tau}},\qquad
 \|r_{N,\tau}\|_{2,\alpha;\rho}\leq C_N\tau^{N+1}.
\]
Dirichlet uniqueness identifies the solutions constructed for different
$N$. Higher Schauder estimates for the difference equation give the same
remainder order in every scaled $C^{k,\alpha}$ norm: keep the highest
derivatives in the elliptic linearization, and use the previously bounded
lower derivatives of the remainder on the right. The initial $C^0$ bound
comes from contraction, rather than from this derivative induction.

For positive $\tau$ ordinary Dirichlet theory gives smooth parameter
dependence. Differentiating the difference equation in $\tau$ contributes
one unweighted surface Laplacian, which costs at most $\rho^{-2}=\tau^{-1}$
in the scaled norms. The highest parameter derivative remains in the
same invertible linearization. Induction, using the higher Schauder
estimates, gives for fixed $k,j$ and sufficiently large $N$
\[
 \|\partial_\tau^j r_{N,\tau}\|_{k,\alpha;\rho}
 \leq C_{N,k,j}\tau^{N+1-j}.
\]
Bundle-parameter derivatives are treated in the same way. Converting
$k$ derivatives to unscaled coordinates loses at most $\rho^{-k}$.
Since $N$ is arbitrary, the desired unscaled remainder and its parameter
derivatives tend to zero at every prescribed finite order. The formal
coefficients are consequently the Taylor coefficients of the exact
family at zero. This proves the smooth extension and the asserted
$O(\tau)$ estimates. All arguments are uniform on the compact family.
An unstable surface fiber in the interior does not change any
Dirichlet hypothesis used here.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The normalized limiting surface operator}
There is a Fredholm problem on the compact base at zero scale.
It keeps the surface transmission equation and fixes the residual
surface gauge at the attaching point. Let $B_-$ and $B_+$ be the
compact negative and positive discs, with common boundary $\Gamma$
and attaching point $z_0=\infty\in\Gamma$. For a seam-admissible
primitive bundle $E$, use its base-flat metric $H_0$. In the unitary
parallel identification from $z_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-base-flat-frame}
 A_B^{(0)}=0,\qquad A_\Sigma^{(0)}=\beta(z),\qquad
 \bar\partial_B\beta^{0,1}=0.
\end{equation}
Only the boundary surface connections are required to be flat.
Put $\alpha=\beta(z_0)$ and $u=u_E$. Write
$H^k_\Sigma=H^k(\Sigma;T^*\Sigma\otimes\ad)$ using the attaching
connection and the complex structure $*_{\Sigma}$. For $q>2$ and
an integer $k\geq0$, define the closed real domain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-surface-domain}
 \begin{split}
 \mathcal X_E^{q,k}=\{(b,\nu)\in{}&
 W^{1,q}(B_-;H^k_\Sigma)\oplus W^{1,q}(B_+;u^*TM_g):\\
 &C_{\beta(z)}b(z)=0,\quad
 H_{\beta(z)}b(z)=\ell_z\nu(z)\quad(z\in\Gamma),\\
 &E_\alpha b(z_0)=0\}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $\ell_z$ gives harmonic representatives. The first Sobolev
norm takes one base derivative and $k$ surface derivatives;
evaluation at $z_0$ is bounded because $q>2$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-surface-operator}
 \begin{split}
 \mathcal Y_E^{q,k}={}&
 L^q(B_-;\Lambda_B^{0,1}\otimes H^k_\Sigma)
 \oplus L^q(B_+;\Lambda_B^{0,1}\otimes u^*TM_g),\\
 \mathcal C_E(b,\nu)={}&(\bar\partial_B b,D_u\nu).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The real form $b$ is identified with its $(0,1)$ part in the first
equation, and $D_u$ is the Dolbeault operator on $u^*TM_g$.
The last row of \eqref{eq:limiting-surface-domain} fixes only the
exact attaching value. Without it,
$(d_{\beta(z)}\zeta,0)$ for base-constant skew-Hermitian $\zeta$
would give an infinite-dimensional kernel.

\begin{proposition}[normalized limiting surface problem]
\label{prop:limiting-surface-inverse}
The operator $\mathcal C_E:\mathcal X_E^{q,k}\to\mathcal Y_E^{q,k}$
is surjective and Fredholm. Its kernel is smooth, and doubling gives
a real-linear isomorphism
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-surface-kernel}
 \ker\mathcal C_E\cong H^1(\PP^1\times\Sigma,\End_0E),
 \qquad \dim_\RR\ker\mathcal C_E=6g-2.
\end{equation}
It has uniformly bounded right inverses on compact smooth families
of these data. For a bounded projection $\Pi_E$ onto its kernel,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-surface-estimate}
 \|v-\Pi_Ev\|_{\mathcal X_E^{q,k}}
             \leq C\|\mathcal C_Ev\|_{\mathcal Y_E^{q,k}}.
\end{equation}
All norms here use the fixed compact base metrics.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We first describe the constant infinite-dimensional boundary problem.
If a complex Hilbert space has a real Lagrangian $L$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:constant-hilbert-disc-inverse}
 \bar\partial:
 \{v\in W^{1,q}(D;L\oplus iL):v|_{\partial D}\in L,
                                      \ v(z_0)=0\}
 \longrightarrow L^q(D;\Lambda^{0,1}\otimes(L\oplus iL))
\end{equation}
is an isomorphism. For scalar data, take a Cauchy--Green solution,
cancel its imaginary boundary trace by a holomorphic correction
obtained from the harmonic Dirichlet solution and its conjugate,
and subtract its remaining real value at $z_0$. The scalar singular
integral and harmonic extension estimates give the $W^{1,q}$
bound. A holomorphic function with real boundary trace is constant,
which proves uniqueness. To extend this bound to Hilbert-valued
data, apply the scalar real-linear inverse to
$\sum_j\epsilon_j f_j$ and average over independent random signs.
The Khintchine inequalities give the bound for pointwise $\ell^2$
norms, also after a base derivative. Density proves
\eqref{eq:constant-hilbert-disc-inverse}.
The corresponding local linear boundary regularity is also covered
by \cite[Theorem~1.3 and Corollary~1.4]{Wehrheim04}, in the author's
corrected version. A separable Hilbert space satisfies its Banach-space
hypothesis through the Rademacher embedding into $L^q$.

Fold the positive disc onto the negative one and choose a unitary
trivialization of $u^*TM_g$, with fiber $V$. The Hilbert space and
complex structure become
\[
 \mathbb H=H^0_\Sigma\oplus V,\qquad
 \mathbb J=(*_\Sigma,-J_V),\qquad \dim_\CC V=3g-3.
\]
The sign accounts for the orientation reversal. The positive
zeroth-order coefficients have finite rank; target identifications
absorb the nonzero conformal factors. Use the harmonic metric on
$V$, so that $\ell_z$ is an isometry. The orthogonal projection
onto the boundary subspace is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-lagrangian-projection}
 P(z)=\begin{pmatrix}
 E_{\beta(z)}+\tfrac12H_{\beta(z)}&\tfrac12\ell_z\\
 \tfrac12\ell_z^*&\tfrac12I_V
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \mathbb JP(z)\mathbb J^{-1}=I-P(z).
\end{equation}
For $G=(d_\beta^*d_\beta)^{-1}$ on surface zero-forms,
$E_\beta=d_\beta Gd_\beta^*$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hodge-projection-derivative}
 \dot E_\beta=\dot d\,Gd^*+dG\dot d^*
                      -dG(\dot d^*d+d^*\dot d)Gd^*.
\end{equation}
Here dotted differentials have surface order zero and
$\dot G=-G(\dot d^*d+d^*\dot d)G$. Each displayed term
maps $H^k_\Sigma$ to $H^{k+1}_\Sigma$: for example
$Gd^*$ gains one derivative, while $dG$ gains one and its
remaining middle factors lose at most one. The same conclusion
holds after every fixed number of base derivatives, by repeated
resolvent differentiation on the compact irreducible family.
The formulas $C_\beta=*E_\beta*^{-1}$ and
$H_\beta=I-E_\beta-C_\beta$ give the other projections.
Thus $P(z)-P(z_0)$ and all its base derivatives gain one
surface derivative. Its harmonic and off-diagonal terms are
smooth finite-rank operators.

Put $P_\infty=P(z_0)$. Choose finite spectral projections $F_N$
containing $V$, the harmonic surface space, and whole attaching
nonharmonic eigenspaces. They commute with $P_\infty$ and
$\mathbb J$. Compactness of the surface operator differences gives
uniform operator-norm convergence of their finite compressions,
including any specified finite collection of base derivatives and
surface Sobolev orders. Hence
\[
 \widetilde P_N=P_\infty+F_N(P-P_\infty)F_N
\]
is close to $P$, self-adjoint, and has the symmetry in
\eqref{eq:limiting-lagrangian-projection}. Spectral projection onto
the cluster near $1$ gives an orthogonal projection $P_N$ with
the same symmetry. Its difference from $P_\infty$ is supported
in $F_N$, and $P_N(z_0)=P_\infty$.

The polar map
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-polar-identification}
 \begin{split}
 S&=PP_N+(I-P)(I-P_N),\qquad U=S(S^*S)^{-1/2},\\
 S-I&=(P-P_N)(2P_N-I),\qquad
 S^*S=I-(P-P_N)^2
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
commutes with $\mathbb J$ and satisfies $UP_NU^{-1}=P$.
It equals $I$ at $z_0$. Its difference from $I$ and all base
derivatives gain one surface derivative: increase $N$ so the
inverse-square-root series converges in each specified Sobolev
operator norm, then use the last row of
\eqref{eq:limiting-polar-identification}. Extend its small logarithm
over a collar and cut it off toward the center of the disc.
This extends the near-identity map $U$, while retaining the
possibly nontrivial finite-dimensional Lagrangian loop $P_N$.

Conjugation by $U$ changes the constant principal operator by a
smooth zeroth-order operator family $K(z)$ gaining one surface
derivative. Multiplication by $K$ is compact from
$W^{1,q}(D;H^k_\Sigma\oplus V)$ to
$L^q(D;H^k_\Sigma\oplus V)$. Indeed, approximate $K$ uniformly
by finite spectral compressions and apply finite-dimensional base
Rellich to each compression. No infinite-dimensional base Rellich
embedding is used. The positive zeroth-order term has the same
compactness property.

The boundary problem for $P_N$ splits into a finite-dimensional
Riemann--Hilbert problem and a constant infinite-dimensional tail.
On that tail the allowed boundary value is exact, so
$E_\alpha b(z_0)=0$ is precisely the normalization in
\eqref{eq:constant-hilbert-disc-inverse}. On the finite part it
imposes only finitely many point conditions. Local complex-linear
straightening, constant real boundary estimates, weak-adjoint
regularity, and finite-dimensional Rellich prove Fredholmness of
the finite part. Compact perturbation now proves Fredholmness
of $\mathcal C_E$.

It also gives smoothness of the kernel. A generalized inverse
for the finite part, together with
\eqref{eq:constant-hilbert-disc-inverse}, preserves each fixed
surface Sobolev order; its kernel is finite-dimensional and smooth.
In the homogeneous equation the term $K$ gains one surface
derivative. Iterating this generalized-inverse identity gives all
surface orders. Differentiating the local constant-boundary estimates
in the base, with smooth coefficients, then gives all base orders.

We prove surjectivity by dense image and the established closed
range. Take smooth targets $f_-,f_+$ supported away from the seam
in their respective discs. Interpret $f_-$ as an $\End_0E$-valued
$(0,2)$ form and lift $f_+$ by the positive harmonic representatives.
Their union is a smooth global $(0,2)$ form $F$ on
$X=\PP^1\times\Sigma$. The vanishing
$H^2(X,\End_0E)=0$ proved in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fredholm-dolbeault} gives a smooth
$(0,1)$ form $v$ with $\bar\partial_Ev=F$. On the positive side
set $\nu(z)=[v_\Sigma(z)]$. The natural Dolbeault operator on
$R^1\pi_*\End_0E=u^*TM_g$ gives $D_u\nu=f_+$; the term
$\bar\partial_\Sigma v_B$ has zero vertical class.

On the boundary there is a unique smooth complex trace-free
potential $\xi$ such that
\[
 v_\Sigma-(\ell\nu)^{0,1}=\bar\partial_\beta\xi.
\]
This is the surface Dolbeault decomposition and simplicity of the
stable boundary fibers. Let $s=(\xi+\xi^*)/2$. In the base-parallel
frame solve the scalar real boundary problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:limiting-real-boundary-correction}
 \bar\partial_B\eta=v_B,\qquad
 \tfrac12(\eta+\eta^*)|_\Gamma=s,
 \qquad b^{0,1}=v_\Sigma-\bar\partial_\beta\eta.
\end{equation}
The same harmonic Dirichlet and conjugate construction used above
is smooth in the surface parameter. Integrability in
\eqref{eq:limiting-base-flat-frame} gives
$\bar\partial_Bb=f_-$. On the boundary $\xi-\eta$ is
skew-Hermitian, so $b=\ell\nu+d_\beta(\xi-\eta)$ and both
seam rows hold. Finally subtract
$(d_{\beta(z)}\zeta,0)$ with
$\zeta=G_\alpha d_\alpha^*b(z_0)$. This is a smooth homogeneous
solution and sets $E_\alpha b(z_0)=0$. Thus every chosen smooth
target is in the image. Such targets are dense in each
$\mathcal Y_E^{q,k}$, proving surjectivity. No bounded extension
of arbitrary transmission traces into a Dolbeault domain is required.

For a kernel element, double the negative cocycle $b^{0,1}$,
whose base component is zero, with the cocycle of the positive
infinitesimal map. As in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fredholm-dolbeault}, subtract the
Dolbeault derivative of an extension of their boundary difference
potential. Equal vertical traces make the piecewise cocycle
distributionally closed; the possible delta term is
$d\bar z$ wedged with the vertical difference, and a jump in the
base component contributes zero. This defines the class in
\eqref{eq:limiting-surface-kernel} independently of choices.

If its class is zero, then $\nu=0$ and the positive cocycle can
be chosen zero. The global exact potential vanishes on the positive
half by stability of its surface fibers. On the negative half one
obtains
\[
 b^{0,1}=\bar\partial_\beta\eta,\qquad
 \bar\partial_B\eta=0,\qquad
 \eta|_\Gamma\text{ skew-Hermitian}.
\]
The Hermitian part is base-harmonic with zero Dirichlet data, hence
zero. A base-holomorphic skew-Hermitian function is base-constant.
Thus $b=d_\beta\zeta$, and the point normalization gives
$d_\alpha\zeta=0$. Irreducibility implies $\zeta=0$, proving
injectivity. Conversely, integrate an $H^1(X,\End_0E)$ class to
a bundle family, using $H^2=0$. Differentiate its smoothly varying
base-flat Dirichlet metric in the parallel identification, together
with its positive map, and normalize by a base-constant surface
gauge. This gives a kernel element doubling to that class. The
dimension is $2(3g-1)$ as computed in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fredholm-dolbeault}.

The smooth kernel bundle has bounded $L^2$ projections on the fixed
compact base. Nearby boundary domains are identified by small polar
maps followed by the homogeneous gauge correction that restores
the point normalization. Bounded evaluation and the smooth surface
Green inverse make these bounded local identifications. Surjectivity
then gives locally bounded right inverses, uniformly over a compact
parameter family. This proves \eqref{eq:limiting-surface-estimate}.
\end{proof}

The harmonic attaching value in this proposition remains free.
The physical operator also contains base one-form components and,
after rescaling, terms $\rho d_\Sigma$. Those terms are unbounded
from $W^{1,q}_BH^k_\Sigma$ to $L^q_BH^k_\Sigma$ at every fixed
$\rho>0$: surface frequency $\lambda$ contributes $\rho\lambda$.
Consequently the proposition does not give a positive-scale inverse
by small bounded perturbation, or the required comparison of compact
and expanding physical norms. We next restore the base equations
on the fixed compact product in a different, fractional norm.

Let $B$ be a fixed smooth compact oriented disc, with
$z_0\in\partial B$. For an irreducible flat surface connection
$\alpha$, put $\Lambda=(d_\alpha^*d_\alpha)^{1/2}$ on trace-free
zero-forms. Write a nonharmonic surface form as
\[
 b=d_\alpha\Lambda^{-1}x+*_\Sigma d_\alpha\Lambda^{-1}y.
\]
If the actual base variation is $ra$, set $m=r\Lambda$.
For $\delta=d_B^*$ and $\operatorname{curl}=*_Bd_B$, a fixed
normalization of the flat ASD--Coulomb rows is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fractional-flat-rows}
 \mathscr D_m(a,x,y)=
 (\delta a+mx,-\operatorname{curl}a+my,dx+*_Bdy-ma).
\end{equation}
Fix $0<\varepsilon<1/2$ and an integer $k\geq0$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fractional-flat-norm}
 \begin{split}
 \|U\|_{\mathcal X_r^{\varepsilon,k}}
  ={}&\|(a,x,y)\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}
      +\|ma\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}
      +\|my\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k},\\
 \|F\|_{\mathcal Y^{\varepsilon,k}}
  ={}&\|(h,v)\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
These are Hilbert tensor Sobolev norms. The graph domain satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fractional-flat-domain}
 \delta a=0,\quad a(n)=0,\quad y|_{\partial B}=0,
 \quad x(z_0)=0.
\end{equation}
Point evaluation is bounded at base order $1+\varepsilon>1$.
There is no $mx$ term in the norm: the weighted surface derivative
of $b$ is its exterior derivative, which controls its coexact part.

\subsection{Fractional cap inverses}
\begin{lemma}[flat fractional estimate]\label{lem:flat-fractional-inverse}
The last two rows of \eqref{eq:fractional-flat-rows} give an
isomorphism $\mathcal A_r:\mathcal X_r^{\varepsilon,k}
\to\mathcal Y^{\varepsilon,k}$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fractional-flat-estimate}
 \|\mathcal A_r^{-1}F\|_{\mathcal X_r^{\varepsilon,k}}
                   \leq C\|F\|_{\mathcal Y^{\varepsilon,k}}
 \qquad(0\leq r\leq1).
\end{equation}
The constant is uniform over compact smooth irreducible attaching
families and over all surface frequencies.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Use the scalar Dirichlet and Neumann Laplacians $\Delta_D,\Delta_N$
on $B$, and the absolute one-form Laplacian $\Delta_{\rm abs}$,
whose boundary conditions are $a(n)=\operatorname{curl}a=0$.
The Dirichlet and absolute spectra have positive gaps because $B$
is a disc. Integration by parts gives
\[
 \mathscr D_m^*(g,h,v)
 =(dg+*_Bdh-mv,mg+\delta v,mh-\operatorname{curl}v),
 \qquad h|_{\partial B}=0,\quad v(n)=0.
\]
The two squares, with their induced boundary domains, are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fractional-flat-squares}
 \begin{split}
 \mathscr D_m^*\mathscr D_m
 &=\operatorname{diag}(\Delta_{\rm abs}+m^2,
                       \Delta_N+m^2,\Delta_D+m^2),\\
 \mathscr D_m\mathscr D_m^*
 &=\operatorname{diag}(\Delta_N+m^2,
                       \Delta_D+m^2,\Delta_{\rm abs}+m^2).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
For example, the extra boundary condition on $x$ is Neumann,
since the normal trace of $dx+*_Bdy-ma$ reduces to $\partial_nx$.
For a target $(0,h,v)$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fractional-flat-resolvent}
 \begin{split}
 H&=(\Delta_D+m^2)^{-1}h,\qquad
 V=(\Delta_{\rm abs}+m^2)^{-1}v,\\
 a&=*_BdH-mV,\qquad x=\delta V,\qquad
 y=mH-\operatorname{curl}V.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Then $\mathscr D_m(a,x,y)=(0,h,v)$, with $a(n)=0$, $y=0$,
and $x$ of base mean zero. Only the two positive-gap resolvents
are used, including at $m=0$.

At order $\varepsilon<1/2$ their spectral Sobolev spaces agree
with ordinary $H^\varepsilon$. At order $1+\varepsilon$, the
Dirichlet and absolute spaces impose $y=0$ and $a(n)=0$,
respectively; the Neumann derivative condition is not imposed
below order $3/2$. These are the noncritical interpolation ranges
of \cite[Definition~2.1 and Theorem~2.2, pp.~833--834]{Grubb16},
including the recalled vector-bundle version. The maps $d,\delta,*$
intertwine the appropriate base Laplacians. On a positive base
eigenspace of eigenvalue $t$, the first-order factors have norm
at most $\sqrt t$, and
\[
 \frac{t}{t+m^2}\leq1,\qquad
 \frac{m\sqrt t}{t+m^2}\leq\frac12,\qquad
 \frac{m^2}{t+m^2}\leq1.
\]
Orthogonal summation over base and surface spectra gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fractional-flat-auxiliary}
 \|(a,x,y)\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}
 +\|m(a,x,y)\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}
                    \leq C\|(h,v)\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}.
\end{equation}

Let $Ta$ be the mean-zero weak Neumann potential defined by
$\int_B\langle dTa,d\zeta\rangle=\int_B\langle a,d\zeta\rangle$.
The base Hodge projection gives $T:H^s\to H^{s+1}$ for the
orders used here. For $\varphi=Ta$ set
\[
 a'=a-d\varphi,\qquad x'=x-m\varphi,\qquad y'=y.
\]
Both ASD rows are unchanged, while $\delta a'=0$ and $a'(n)=0$.
The estimate for $x'$ uses
\[
 \|m\varphi\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}
             \leq C\|ma\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k};
\]
the weighted bound for $a'$ uses the bounded base Hodge projection
on $H^\varepsilon$. Thus \eqref{eq:fractional-flat-auxiliary}
controls \eqref{eq:fractional-flat-norm}. Subtract the base constant
$x'(z_0)$ to impose the point condition. This is bounded by point
evaluation and changes neither ASD row nor either weighted term.
It would not give a bound for an additional $mx'$ term, which is
why that term is omitted.

For uniqueness at a fixed positive surface mass, the Neumann
gauge equation $\Delta_N+m^2$ puts a homogeneous solution into
the full Coulomb domain. It then vanishes by
\eqref{eq:fractional-flat-squares}. The original solution is pure
gauge, $a=d\psi$, $x=m\psi$, $y=0$. Base Coulomb gauge makes
$\psi$ constant, and $x(z_0)=0$ removes it. This is a modewise
argument and uses no uniform bound for that gauge equation.
At $r=0$, the base divergence-curl problem has zero kernel and
the real Cauchy--Riemann kernel consists of constants removed at
$z_0$. The graph domain is complete by closedness of the surface
operators and continuity of the traces. Finite surface-mode
approximation extends the construction to that domain.
\end{proof}

Return to the exact compact reconstruction $(A_{E,\rho},u_E)$.
Use the fixed smooth metric $\rho^2g_B+g_\Sigma$ throughout the
next proposition. For $0<\varepsilon<1/2$ and integer $k\geq2$,
write the actual negative variation as $\rho a+b$ and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-fractional-graph}
 \begin{split}
 \|U\|_{X_\rho}={}&
 \|(a,b,\nu)\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}
 +\rho\|d_\alpha a\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}
 +\rho\|d_\alpha b\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k},\\
 \delta_Ba={}&0,\quad a(n)=0,\quad
 C_{\beta_\rho}b=0,\quad H_{\beta_\rho}b=\ell_\rho\nu
 \quad\text{on }\Gamma,\quad E_\alpha b(z_0)=0.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The positive summand has only its finite-dimensional base norm.
The weighted derivative of $b$ is exterior differentiation.
Let $Y$ be the $H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k$ space of normalized
negative scalar and mixed rows, together with the positive
$H^\varepsilon$ target.

\begin{proposition}[uniform compact estimate]
\label{prop:compact-fractional-inverse}
On a compact smooth seam-admissible primitive bundle family there
are unitary representatives with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-base-gauge}
 \delta_B A_{E,\rho,B}=0,\quad A_{E,\rho,B}(n)=0,\quad
 A_{E,\rho,B}=O(\rho^2),\quad
 \beta_\rho=\beta_0+O(\rho^2),\quad \beta_\rho(z_0)=\alpha.
\end{equation}
The bounds hold in every fixed smooth norm, with bundle parameters.
In these representatives, the normalized linearized mixed operator
$\mathcal A_{E,\rho}:X_\rho\to Y$ is surjective Fredholm of
real index $6g-2$, for $0\leq\rho\leq\rho_0$. Its smooth kernel
is naturally $H^1(X,\End_0E)$. There are uniformly bounded kernel
projections $\Pi_{E,\rho}$ and right inverses satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-fractional-inverse}
 \|U-\Pi_{E,\rho}U\|_{X_\rho}
                    \leq C\|\mathcal A_{E,\rho}U\|_Y.
\end{equation}
At zero scale the base variable is the independent base
divergence-curl block. The harmonic attaching value remains free.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The smooth compact metric family gives $A_{E,\rho,B}=O(\rho^2)$
in the base-flat parallel trivialization. Apply the ordinary base
Neumann Hodge potential $T$ pointwise on $\Sigma$. In logarithmic
gauge coordinates the equation is
$\chi=-T(A_B+N(A_B,\chi))$ on mean-zero potentials, with $N$
quadratic. Fixed-base H\"older estimates give a contraction in a
small $C^{j+1,\gamma}$ ball and $\chi=O(\rho^2)$. Regularity and
parameter differentiation give this at every order. This is a
two-dimensional problem with smooth surface parameters. Multiply
the gauge by its value at $z_0$ inverse; base-constant conjugation
preserves the base conditions and makes the gauge the identity at
$z_0$. This proves \eqref{eq:compact-base-gauge}.

First extend Proposition~\ref{prop:limiting-surface-inverse} to
$H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k\to H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k$.
The scalar Cauchy--Green inverse has this bound. Its real boundary
correction uses harmonic extension of an $H^{1/2+\varepsilon}$
trace, and its point subtraction is bounded. Hilbert summation
gives the constant infinite-dimensional inverse. Smooth local
complex frames reduce the finite Lagrangian boundary problem to
that scalar boundary estimate with zeroth-order terms. A finite
partition, the interior estimate, and the corresponding adjoint
estimate give Fredholmness and smooth homogeneous kernels.
Its finite point conditions are imposed at $H^{1+\varepsilon}$,
where evaluation is bounded. The smoothing remainders in the
proof of that proposition are compact by finite surface compression
followed by $H^{1+\varepsilon}(B)\Subset H^\varepsilon(B)$.
Since $\varepsilon<1/2$, smooth targets supported away from the
seam are dense in $H^\varepsilon$. The same smooth Dolbeault
construction gives dense image, hence surjectivity by closed range,
and identifies the kernel with $H^1(X,\End_0E)$. The base block
$-\operatorname{curl}$ on $\delta_Ba=0$, $a(n)=0$ is an
isomorphism with inverse $*_Bd\Delta_D^{-1}$, so adds index zero.
This proves the zero-scale assertion separately.

Use the finite Lagrangian projection $P_N$ and the complex-linear
polar map $U$ in the preceding limiting proof. Include in its
finite spectral space the corresponding surface zero-form modes
for $a$. The positive-scale boundary projection differs by
$O(\rho^2)$ from its limit, with one surface derivative gain.
A second polar map $U_\rho$, equal to $I$ at $z_0$, identifies
this domain with the limit domain. The composite $W_\rho=U_\rho U$
maps the $P_N$ domain to the actual surface/positive domain and
leaves $a$ unchanged. Both maps and their inverses preserve the
graph norms uniformly: $W_\rho-I$ gains one surface derivative,
so $\rho d_\alpha(W_\rho-I)$ and
$(W_\rho^{-1}-I)\rho d_\alpha$ are $O(\rho)$ maps of surface
order zero. Off-diagonal positive components are finite rank with
smooth surface range. The point condition is unchanged.

Let $R_\rho$ have constant-$\alpha$ flat differential expression,
constant harmonic/positive Cauchy--Riemann expression, and boundary
projection $P_N$. Its infinite tail has the uniform inverse of
Lemma~\ref{lem:flat-fractional-inverse}. Its finite part is
$R_{0,N}+\rho B_N$ on a fixed base domain, with $B_N$ bounded.
If $a_N,c_N$ are its zero-scale kernel and cokernel dimensions,
choose smooth columns $F:\RR^{c_N}\to Y_N$ spanning the cokernel
and bounded coordinates $L:X_N\to\RR^{a_N}$ on the smooth
kernel. Smooth columns exist by density; no regularity of cokernel
functionals is needed. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-reference-stabilization}
 T_\rho(U,\eta)=(R_\rho U+F\eta,LU)
\end{equation}
is invertible at zero and remains uniformly invertible on the
finite part for small $\rho$. Together with the tail estimate,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-reference-bound}
 \|T_\rho^{-1}\|_{Y\oplus\RR^{a_N}
                    \to X_\rho\oplus\RR^{c_N}}\leq C.
\end{equation}
Here and below, until returning by $W_\rho$, $X_\rho$ denotes
the reference graph domain with projection $P_N$.

For clarity, compare the varying graph spaces in a fixed product:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-graph-embedding}
 J_\rho U=(U,\rho d_\alpha a,\rho d_\alpha b)
                        \in X_0\oplus Y\oplus Y.
\end{equation}
For each fixed target $f$, the vectors
$(J_\rho,I)T_\rho^{-1}f$ converge to $(U_0,0,0,\eta_0)$,
where $(U_0,\eta_0)=T_0^{-1}f$. Indeed, for finitely many surface
modes this is bounded perturbation on a fixed base problem, and
the weighted derivatives tend to zero. Such targets are dense;
\eqref{eq:compact-reference-bound} extends the convergence to
every target. This is strong convergence on fixed sources.

The normalized negative rows of the exact operator, before the
boundary identification, have the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-normalized-rows}
 \begin{split}
 S_\rho(a,b)&=-*_B(d_Ba+[A_{E,\rho,B}\wedge a])
                           -\rho *_\Sigma d_{\beta_\rho}b,\\
 M_\rho(a,b)&=(d_Bb+[A_{E,\rho,B},b]
                              -\rho d_{\beta_\rho}a)^{0,2}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The scalar row is the negative contracted variation of
$F_{BB}+\rho^2F_{\Sigma\Sigma}$ divided by $\rho$, with base
variation $\rho a$. Indeed $*_\Sigma d_\alpha b=-\Lambda y$
in the flat coexact component, giving $-\operatorname{curl}a+my$.
The mixed row
has no further singular division. Thus $\beta_\rho-\alpha$ enters
only as $\rho$ times smooth multiplication, and the base connection
as $O(\rho^2)$ multiplication.

Conjugate the mixed target by $W_\rho^{-1}$. Complex linearity
preserves the base principal symbol. The derivatives of $U$ and
the finite-dimensional positive connection give a fixed term $K$
gaining one surface derivative. All other terms just described,
including those with $\rho d_\alpha(U-I)$, give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-parameter-decomposition}
 \widehat{\mathcal A}_{E,\rho}=R_\rho+K+S_\rho,
 \qquad K:X_0\to Y\text{ compact},\qquad
 \|S_\rho\|_{X_\rho\to Y}\leq C\rho.
\end{equation}
Compactness again uses finite surface approximation before base
Rellich. Augment with $F,L$ and compose with $T_\rho^{-1}$.
The result is the identity plus a compact operator plus an operator
of norm $O(\rho)$. Absorbing the last term proves Fredholmness
of index zero for the augmented exact operator. Its unaugmented
index is $a_N-c_N=6g-2$, by the zero-scale result and compact
perturbation invariance. The unbounded surface perturbation has
never been treated as a bounded map on $X_0$.

We next identify the kernel without using the uniform estimate.
For fixed $\rho>0$, $k\geq2$ implies $X_\rho\subset W^{1,2}$
on the four-dimensional product. A fixed-scale covariant Neumann
solve puts a homogeneous field into full Coulomb gauge with zero
normal component. It preserves ASD and matching, so
Proposition~\ref{prop:fredholm-dolbeault} gives a smooth
representative of a class in $H^1(X,\End_0E)$. To return a smooth
representative $v$ to the base gauge, solve
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-kernel-base-gauge}
 \Delta_B\chi+\delta_B[A_{E,\rho,B},\chi]=-\delta_Bv_B,
 \qquad \partial_n\chi=-v_B(n).
\end{equation}
The compatibility condition is the divergence theorem. Pointwise
on $\Sigma$, the mean-zero Neumann operator is an invertible small
perturbation of $\Delta_B$. Its remaining kernel consists exactly
of base-constant surface potentials, since $A_{E,\rho,B}$ has
zero base divergence and normal trace. Such a potential changes
$b(z_0)$ by $d_\alpha\zeta$; the unique choice
$\zeta=-G_\alpha d_\alpha^*b(z_0)$ imposes the point condition.
The same reasoning proves regularity of an initially rough field:
after subtracting the smooth solution of the base equation, its
potential is base-constant as a distribution, and the point
normalization identifies it with a smooth surface Green solution.
It does not evaluate an arbitrary $W^{2,2}$ potential at $z_0$.
Thus the kernel has real dimension $6g-2$ and the cokernel is zero.

For the kernel projections, integrate a basis of $H^1(X,\End_0E)$
to a local bundle deformation family using $H^2=0$. Differentiate
its smooth compact Dirichlet reconstruction in the base gauge.
Normalize the attaching connection in a local surface Coulomb
slice, or use the base-constant correction after differentiating.
The resulting kernel basis satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:compact-kernel-basis-limit}
 e_{\rho,j}=(a_{\rho,j},b_{\rho,j},\nu_j),\qquad
 a_{\rho,j}=O(\rho),\qquad b_{\rho,j}\longrightarrow b_{0,j}
                         \quad\text{in }C^\infty.
\end{equation}
The point correction changes the normalized base component by
$\rho^{-1}[A_{E,\rho,B},\zeta]=O(\rho)$. The basis therefore
converges in the fixed graph embedding, with weighted components
tending to zero. Its unweighted $L^2$ Gram matrix stays invertible,
giving uniformly bounded projections that converge in the fixed
base norms. This construction also holds at rank drop and in
genus one; it uses the unobstructed bundle family, not constant
rank of the derivative of the positive map.

Suppose now that \eqref{eq:compact-fractional-inverse} fails for
$\rho_j\to0$. In the reference domain choose $U_j$ of graph norm
one with $\Pi_{E,\rho_j}U_j=0$ and
$\widehat{\mathcal A}_{E,\rho_j}U_j\to0$. The identity
\[
 T_{\rho_j}(U_j,0)=
 (\widehat{\mathcal A}_{E,\rho_j}U_j-KU_j-S_{\rho_j}U_j,LU_j)
\]
has a convergent subsequence on the right: $K$ is compact,
$S_{\rho_j}U_j\to0$, and $L$ is finite dimensional. Strong
convergence of the stabilized inverses and their uniform bound
imply $J_{\rho_j}U_j\to(U_0,0,0)$, with $\|U_0\|_{X_0}=1$.
Passing to the operator and to the independently convergent kernel
projections gives $\mathcal A_{E,0}U_0=0$ and $\Pi_{E,0}U_0=0$,
a contradiction. A convergent sequence of bundle parameters proves
uniformity on compact families. Surjectivity and the estimate give
the right inverse on the projected complement. Returning by the
uniform domain identifications completes the proof.
\end{proof}

This proposition concerns a fixed smooth compact base metric.
The Euclidean dilation metric is singular at its compactifying
point. Replacing it by a scale-dependent smooth cap introduces a
variable surface coefficient, which is not covered by the
constant-mass diagonalization above. The following estimates treat
that coefficient explicitly. They still leave the outer attaching
equations and the global nonlinear problem to be solved.

Let $f\geq1$ be smooth on the fixed disc $B$, and set
\[
 M=\|f\|_\infty,\qquad L=1+\|d\log f\|_\infty.
\]
Use the metric $\rho^2f^2g_B+g_\Sigma$ and the base variation
$\rho a$, so $m=\rho\Lambda$ remains constant in the base.
For the nonharmonic surface coordinates $x,y$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variable-factor-flat-rows}
 \mathcal A_{\rho,f}(a,x,y)
 =\bigl(f^{-1}(-\operatorname{curl}a+mf^2y),
                         dx+*_Bdy-ma\bigr)=(H,v).
\end{equation}
The unweighted scalar numerator is $h=fH$. Its invariant form is
$-*_Bd_Ba-\rho f^2 *_\Sigma d_\alpha b$. Impose
\eqref{eq:fractional-flat-domain}, and use the complete graph norm
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variable-factor-flat-norm}
 \begin{split}
 \|U\|_{X_{\rho,f}^{\varepsilon,k}}={}&
 \|(x,y)\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}
 +M^{-1}\|a\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}\\
 &+\|ma\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}
 +\|mf y\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Euclidean cap metrics and their inverse}
\begin{lemma}[variable conformal factor]\label{lem:variable-factor-inverse}
For $0<\varepsilon<1/2$ and integer $k\geq0$,
\eqref{eq:variable-factor-flat-rows} is an isomorphism from
\eqref{eq:variable-factor-flat-norm} to the $H_B^\varepsilon
H_\Sigma^k$ target, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variable-factor-estimate}
 \|\mathcal A_{\rho,f}^{-1}F\|_{X_{\rho,f}^{\varepsilon,k}}
                    \leq C L^\varepsilon\|F\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}.
\end{equation}
The constant is independent of $\rho\geq0$, all surface
frequencies, $M$, and higher derivatives of $f$. It is uniform
on compact smooth irreducible attaching families. The factor
$M^{-1}$ in the base norm is part of this assertion.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Work first in one surface mode. The base Hodge decomposition is
\[
 v=dx_0+*_Bdz,\qquad \int_Bx_0=0,\qquad z|_{\partial B}=0,
 \qquad x_0=Tv,\quad z=-\Delta_D^{-1}\operatorname{curl}v.
\]
The maps have their usual $H^s\to H^{s+1}$ bounds. No boundary
condition on $v$ is imposed; $Tv$ uses its natural Neumann data.
Write $a=*_Bd\psi$, $\psi|_{\partial B}=0$, by base Coulomb
gauge. The equations become
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variable-factor-stream}
 \begin{split}
 x&=x_0+\text{constant},\qquad y-m\psi=z,\\
 (\Delta_D+m^2f^2)\psi&=fH-mf^2z,\qquad
 (\Delta_D+m^2f^2)y=\Delta z+mfH.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The zero Dirichlet traces remove the constant in $y-m\psi=z$.
The positive Dirichlet operator supplies existence, including at
$m=0$. At the fractional order, set $x=x_0-x_0(z_0)$.

Pair the last equation with $y$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variable-factor-energy}
 \|dy\|_2^2+\|mf y\|_2^2
 =\langle dz,dy\rangle+\langle H,mf y\rangle.
\end{equation}
Dirichlet Poincar\'e, the base Hodge bound, and
$ma=*_Bd(y-z)$ give
\[
 \|y\|_{H^1}+\|mf y\|_2+\|ma\|_2\leq C\|(H,v)\|_2.
\]
Since $\Delta\psi=f(H-mf y)$, Dirichlet regularity also gives
$M^{-1}\|a\|_{H^1}\leq C\|(H,v)\|_2$. These estimates are
independent of $f,m$, with mean normalization for $x_0$.

For the next endpoint take $H\in H^1_0(B)$ and $v\in H^1(B;T^*B)$.
Pair the last equation of \eqref{eq:variable-factor-stream} with
$\Delta y$. Integration by parts gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:variable-factor-differentiated-energy}
 \begin{split}
 \|\Delta y\|_2^2+\|mf\,dy\|_2^2
 ={}&\langle\Delta z,\Delta y\rangle
       +m\langle d(fH),dy\rangle
       -2m^2\langle fy\,df,dy\rangle.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
The scalar source boundary term vanishes because $H$ has zero
trace. The last two terms are bounded by
\[
 \bigl(\|dH\|_2+L\|H\|_2+2L\|mf y\|_2\bigr)\|mf\,dy\|_2.
\]
Absorption and the preceding energy bound imply
\[
 \|y\|_{H^2}+\|mf\,dy\|_2\leq CL\|(H,v)\|_{H^1}.
\]
Differentiating $mf y$ adds $(d\log f)(mf y)$, so its $H^1$
norm has the same bound, as does $ma=*_Bd(y-z)$. Moreover
\[
 \|f(H-mf y)\|_{H^1}
 \leq M\bigl(\|H-mf y\|_{H^1}+L\|H-mf y\|_2\bigr)
 \leq CML\|(H,v)\|_{H^1}.
\]
Thus $M^{-1}\|a\|_{H^2}$ has the same $CL$ bound.

Interpolate the same linear inverse, still with mean-normalized
$x_0$, between the source spaces
$L^2\oplus L^2(T^*B)$ and $H^1_0\oplus H^1(T^*B)$.
For $\varepsilon<1/2$ their interpolation space is ordinary
$H^\varepsilon\oplus H^\varepsilon(T^*B)$, with no boundary
condition; this is the noncritical Dirichlet range in
\cite[Definition~2.1 and Theorem~2.2]{Grubb16}.
Interpolate the fixed output product
$(x_0,y,M^{-1}a,ma,mf y)$ at orders $(1,1,1,0,0)$ and
$(2,2,2,1,1)$. The constants $C$ and $CL$ give $CL^\varepsilon$.
The final point subtraction is bounded because $1+\varepsilon>1$;
it is not made at the $H^1$ endpoint. Summing both endpoint
estimates over surface modes, with the commuting $H^k$ weights,
before interpolation proves the Hilbert-valued assertion.

For uniqueness the homogeneous Hodge data have $x=z=0$, and
the first Dirichlet equation in
\eqref{eq:variable-factor-stream} gives $\psi=0$, hence $a=y=0$.
Closedness of $\Lambda$ and $f\Lambda$, together with continuity
of the traces, gives completeness. Finite surface-mode density
extends the inverse to the stated domain. Its forward operator
norm may depend on $M,L$; only the displayed inverse bound is
asserted uniformly.
\end{proof}

We now construct the backgrounds to which this estimate will be
applied. Near $z_0=\infty$, use $\zeta=1/w$ and write the
Euclidean conformal factor as $f_\infty=c(\zeta)|\zeta|^{-2}$,
with $c$ smooth and positive. Choose the fixed $g_B$ sufficiently
small that $f_\infty\geq2$. For a smooth cutoff $\chi$ equal to
zero on $[0,1]$ and one on $[2,\infty)$, set near $z_0$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:euclidean-cap-factor}
 f_R=c(\zeta)\bigl(\chi(R|\zeta|)|\zeta|^{-2}
                       +(1-\chi(R|\zeta|))R^2\bigr),
\end{equation}
and use $f_\infty$ away from this chart. For large $R$ it is
smooth, is at least one, and equals $f_\infty$ on $|w|\leq R/2$.
Rescaling the transition annulus gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:euclidean-cap-bounds}
 \|f_R\|_\infty\leq CR^2,\qquad
 |D^j f_R|\leq C_jR^j f_R,\qquad
 1+\|d\log f_R\|_\infty\leq CR.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Dirichlet metrics for the caps]\label{lem:cap-dirichlet-metric}
For $R\leq\rho^{-1/6}$ and sufficiently small $\rho$, the
Dirichlet reconstruction for $\rho^2f_R^2g_B+g_\Sigma$ satisfies,
at every specified finite derivative order,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-metric-unscaled}
 \|\log(H_0^{-1}H_{\rho,R})\|_{C^j}
                              \leq C_j\rho^2R^{4+j}.
\end{equation}
For fixed $J$, when $\rho^2R^{J+8}$ is sufficiently small it
admits base-gauged unitary representatives with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-background-bound}
 \begin{split}
 &\delta_BA_B=0,\quad A_B(n)=0,\quad \beta(z_0)=\alpha,\\
 &\|A_B\|_{C^J}+\|\beta-\beta_0\|_{C^J}
                                      \leq C_J\rho^2R^{J+8}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
They satisfy the canonical matching with $u_E$. Constants are
uniform on compact smooth seam-admissible bundle families; a finite
number of bundle-parameter derivatives is obtained by increasing
the derivative order used in the estimate.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $\tau=\rho^2$ and $\eta=R^{-1}$. The normalized equation is
\[
 \Phi_{\tau,R}(H)=i\Lambda_BF_H^\perp
             +\tau f_R^2 i\Lambda_\Sigma F_H^\perp=0,
 \qquad H|_{\partial B}=H_0|_{\partial B}.
\]
Use base charts of radius $c\eta$ and surface charts of radius
$c\eta\rho f_R(z)$ at the base center $z$, including boundary
charts. Their pulled-back H\"older norms contain the supremum
norm and have uniform product constants.
Equation~\eqref{eq:euclidean-cap-bounds} controls the ratio
$f_R/f_R(z)$ and all its rescaled derivatives on each chart.
The equation multiplied by $\eta^2$ is uniformly elliptic there.

The transported metric linearization is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-metric-linearization}
 L_{K,\tau,R}=\tfrac12\bigl(\nabla_{K,B}^*\nabla_{K,B}
                  +\tau f_R^2\nabla_{K,\Sigma}^*\nabla_{K,\Sigma}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Indeed, apply the K\"ahler identity to the actual metric
$\tau f_R^2g_B+g_\Sigma$, cancel the transported commutators,
and multiply its covariant Laplacian by $\tau f_R^2$.
The base volume factor cancels the inverse base metric, and the
surface derivative does not differentiate $f_R$. Thus no derivative
of $f_R$ occurs in \eqref{eq:cap-metric-linearization}.
Kato's inequality and the fixed base barrier give
$\|s\|_\infty\leq C\|L_{K,\tau,R}s\|_\infty$ for zero
Dirichlet data. If $K$ has bounded adapted derivatives, local
Schauder estimates therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-metric-adapted-inverse}
 \|s\|_{j+2,\gamma;\mathrm{ad}}
 \leq C_j\bigl(\|s\|_\infty+\eta^2
                    \|L_{K,\tau,R}s\|_{j,\gamma;\mathrm{ad}}\bigr)
 \leq C_j\|L_{K,\tau,R}s\|_{j,\gamma;\mathrm{ad}}.
\end{equation}
The fixed-positive-parameter index-zero Dirichlet argument supplies
surjectivity. Undoing the factor $\eta^2$ in the nonlinear
equation gives a quadratic constant at most $CR^2$.

In the base-flat identification the zero-scale inverse is the
scalar fixed-base Dirichlet inverse. Construct formal coefficients
$s_i$, zero on the boundary, for
$H^{[N]}=H_0\exp(\sum_{i=1}^N\tau^is_i)$. They obey
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-formal-coefficients}
 \|s_i\|_{C^j}\leq C_{i,j}R^{6i-2+j}.
\end{equation}
For $i=1$ the source is $f_R^2i\Lambda_\Sigma F_{H_0}^\perp$,
of size $R^{4+j}$. At higher order, a base nonlinear monomial
has at least $q=2$ positive-order factors and at most two
derivatives, so its exponent is at most $6i-2q+j+2\leq6i-2+j$.
A surface monomial has the extra $\tau f_R^2$, giving
$4+6(i-1)-2q+j+2\leq6i-2+j$ for $q\geq1$.
The base inverse preserves the fixed $C^j$ estimate, with no
surface derivative loss. This proves the induction.

In the adapted charts every derivative of these coefficients is
bounded by the same power $R^{6i-2}$: base derivatives have factor
$R^{-1}$, and surface derivatives cost at most $C\rho R^2$
after \eqref{eq:cap-formal-coefficients}. One more derivative
controls the H\"older seminorm. Consequently $H^{[N]}$ and its
inverse have bounded adapted derivatives when $\rho^2R^6$ is
small. The uncancelled residual is bounded by
\[
 e_N=C_N\rho^{2N+2}R^{6N+4}
\]
at every fixed adapted order. The same monomial count bounds
the analytic tail by a convergent power series in $\rho^2R^6$.
Using \eqref{eq:cap-metric-adapted-inverse}, contraction has
smallness $R^2e_N\leq C_N(\rho^2R^6)^{N+1}$ and gives an exact
correction $r_N$ of adapted size $Ce_N$. Higher adapted Schauder
estimates give this bound at every fixed order; the supremum
bound is already supplied by contraction. Dirichlet uniqueness
identifies the solutions obtained with different $N$.

Unscaling $j$ derivatives costs at most $(R/\rho)^j$, hence
\[
 \|r_N\|_{C^j}\leq C_{N,j}\rho^{2N+2-j}R^{6N+4+j}.
\]
For $R\leq\rho^{-1/6}$ its ratio to $\rho^2R^{4+j}$ is at
most $\rho^{N-j}$. Choose $N\geq j+1$ and use the finite sum
\eqref{eq:cap-formal-coefficients} to prove
\eqref{eq:cap-metric-unscaled}. This is a finite-order statement;
it does not assert smallness of all derivatives at one fixed
growth exponent.

The connection formula uses one derivative, giving
$\|A_B\|_{C^j}+\|\beta-\beta_0\|_{C^j}
\leq C_j\rho^2R^{5+j}$ before base gauge. Apply the ordinary
base Neumann gauge construction, treating surface variables as
parameters. At parameter order $J+1$ its contraction controls
$\chi,d_B\chi$ in $C^{J+1,\gamma}$; no surface derivative
occurs in that gauge equation. The extra parameter derivative
controls $d_\Sigma\chi$ in the transformed surface connection.
An integer $C^{J+2}$ norm of $A_B$ suffices, supplied by metric
order $J+3$ with size $\rho^2R^{J+7}$. Allowing one further
power gives \eqref{eq:cap-background-bound}. The final
base-constant normalization makes the gauge equal to the identity
at $z_0$ and preserves the two base conditions. The Dirichlet
surface metric supplies the exact canonical matching.
\end{proof}

For the full mixed cap use the closed domain in
\eqref{eq:compact-fractional-graph}, with its nonflat matching,
but replace its norm by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:capped-mixed-graph}
 \begin{split}
 \|U\|_{X_{\rho,f}}={}&
 \|(b,\nu)\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}
 +M^{-1}\|a\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k}\\
 &+\rho\|d_\alpha a\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}
 +\rho\|f d_\alpha b\|_{H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Normalize the scalar target by $f^{-1}$ as in
\eqref{eq:variable-factor-flat-rows}; the mixed and positive
targets retain their complex base form. Denote their
$H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^k$ space by $Y$.

\begin{proposition}[mixed inverse on the Euclidean caps]
\label{prop:capped-mixed-inverse}
Fix $0<\varepsilon<1/2$ and integer $k\geq2$. Choose a fixed
sufficiently large derivative order $J$ for the surface Hodge
and boundary identifications. For
\begin{equation}\label{eq:capped-radius-range}
 0<a<\min\{1/6,2/(J+10+2\varepsilon)\},\qquad R=\rho^{-a},
\end{equation}
the exact capped reconstruction has a surjective normalized mixed
operator $\mathcal A_{\rho,f_R}:X_{\rho,f_R}\to Y$, of index
$6g-2$ and smooth kernel $H^1(X,\End_0E)$. It has a kernel
projection $\Pi$ and right inverse $Q$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:capped-mixed-inverse-bound}
 \|U-\Pi U\|_{X_{\rho,f_R}}\leq CR^\varepsilon
                           \|\mathcal A_{\rho,f_R}U\|_Y,
 \qquad \|Q\|+\|\Pi\|\leq CR^\varepsilon.
\end{equation}
Constants are uniform on compact smooth seam-admissible bundle
families. The harmonic attaching value remains free.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We prove a criterion for a general $f\geq1$. Suppose its exact
base-gauged background has
$\|A_B\|_{C^J}+\|\beta-\beta_0\|_{C^J}\leq\delta$ and the
same attaching connection. It suffices that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:capped-inverse-smallness}
 \rho ML^{4\varepsilon}+\delta ML^{2\varepsilon}<c.
\end{equation}
Multiplication by $f/M$ and $1/f$ has $H^\varepsilon$ norm at
most $CL^\varepsilon$: interpolate their $L^2$ bound one and
their $H^1$ bound $CL$.

Make the fixed limiting boundary reduction once, with projection
$P_{N_0}$ and polar map $U$. A second polar map differing from
$I$ by $O(\delta)$ identifies the exact boundary; both are the
identity at $z_0$. Their composite acts on $b,\nu$ and leaves
$a$ unchanged. The smoothing commutators with the mass terms
are bounded by $C\rho ML^\varepsilon$ in
\eqref{eq:capped-mixed-graph}, so these are uniform domain
identifications under \eqref{eq:capped-inverse-smallness}.
The exact normalized rows before identification are
\[
 -f^{-1}*_B(d_Ba+[A_B\wedge a])-\rho f *_\Sigma d_\beta b,
 \qquad(d_Bb+[A_B,b]-\rho d_\beta a)^{0,2}.
\]
Conjugating the mixed target gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:capped-operator-decomposition}
 \widehat{\mathcal A}_{\rho,f}=R_{\rho,f}+K+\mathcal E_{\rho,f},
 \qquad\|\mathcal E_{\rho,f}\|_{X_{\rho,f}\to Y}
                         \leq CML^\varepsilon(\rho+\delta).
\end{equation}
Here $R_{\rho,f}$ has the flat variable-factor expression and
boundary $P_{N_0}$. The fixed term $K$ gains one surface derivative
and acts only on $b,\nu$. The term $f^{-1}[A_B,a]$ costs
$C\delta ML^\varepsilon\|a/M\|$, and the two surface connection
differences cost $C\rho ML^\varepsilon$. The remaining terms
are the derivatives of the small polar map and its smoothing
mass commutators, giving the stated bound.

For a surface cutoff $N\geq N_0$, let $F_N$ contain the positive,
harmonic, and complete exact/coexact modes up to frequency $N$,
with corresponding zero-form modes for $a$. It commutes with the
reference boundary and expression. The fixed smoothing term obeys
\[
 K_N=F_NKF_N,\qquad
 \|K-K_N\|_{H_B^{1+\varepsilon}H_\Sigma^k\to Y}\leq C/N.
\]
The right truncation uses $K:H^{k-1}_\Sigma\to H^k_\Sigma$,
the left one $K:H^k_\Sigma\to H^{k+1}_\Sigma$.
There is no factor $M$ because $K$ does not act on $a$.
Let $d=6g-2$ and choose fixed smooth coordinates $\ell$ on
the limiting surface kernel, so its operator augmented by $\ell$
is an isomorphism. The coordinates $\ell_N=\ell F_N$ converge
in operator norm. Thus $(R_0^{\rm surface}+K_N,\ell_N)$ is
invertible uniformly for all sufficiently large $N$, by the
already proved limiting theorem. Its high tail is the constant
real boundary isomorphism, so the finite part has that uniform
bound as well.

The zero-scale base inverse is $a=*_Bd\Delta_D^{-1}(fH)$,
bounded by $CL^\varepsilon$ in the $M^{-1}$-weighted norm.
On the finite space, the positive mass perturbation has norm
$C\rho MN L^\varepsilon$, and the extra graph terms have this
bound relative to the zero-scale norm. Hence, if
$\rho MN L^{2\varepsilon}$ is small, the finite augmented
inverse has bound $CL^\varepsilon$ in the full graph norm.
On the infinite tail apply Lemma~\ref{lem:variable-factor-inverse}.
Their direct sum
\[
 T_{\rho,f,N}U=(R_{\rho,f}U+K_NU,\ell_NU)
\]
is therefore invertible with norm $CL^\varepsilon$.

Choose $N$ comparable to $A L^{2\varepsilon}$, with $A$ a
sufficiently large fixed constant and $N\geq N_0$. After this
reference inverse the error in the exact augmented operator is
bounded by
\[
 C\bigl(L^\varepsilon/N+ML^{2\varepsilon}(\rho+\delta)\bigr).
\]
The choice of $A$ controls its first term.
Condition~\eqref{eq:capped-inverse-smallness} controls the others
and ensures the finite perturbation is small. A Neumann series
gives an exact augmented inverse $B=(\widehat{\mathcal A}_{\rho,f},
\ell_N)^{-1}$ with norm $CL^\varepsilon$. Set
$QF=B(F,0)$ and $\Pi U=B(0,\ell_NU)$. These give surjectivity,
kernel dimension $d$, and the stated estimate. At fixed $\rho,f$,
the regularity and base-gauge argument in
Proposition~\ref{prop:compact-fractional-inverse} identifies the
smooth kernel with $H^1(X,\End_0E)$.

Finally \eqref{eq:euclidean-cap-bounds} and
\eqref{eq:cap-background-bound} bound the two smallness terms
by $C\rho R^{2+4\varepsilon}$ and
$C\rho^2R^{J+10+2\varepsilon}$. Both tend to zero in
\eqref{eq:capped-radius-range}. Also $L^\varepsilon\leq CR^\varepsilon$,
which proves \eqref{eq:capped-mixed-inverse-bound}.
\end{proof}

The cap agrees with the physical Euclidean metric on $|w|\leq R/2$.
Its outer metric is auxiliary. Proposition~\ref{prop:gluing-geometric-inverse}
combines this cap inverse with compatible cutoffs, nonzero neck
data and the residual inverse on the full preglued configuration.
The following proposition first gives the nonlinear chart on
the cap. The physical comparison used in the converse is proved
in Section~\ref{sec:regular-converse}.

\subsection{Nonlinear coordinates on an exact cap}
\begin{proposition}[nonlinear coordinates on the cap]
\label{prop:nonlinear-cap-chart}
Use the background, domain, and norm of
Proposition~\ref{prop:capped-mixed-inverse}. There are uniform
local coordinates on the nonlinear canonical matching domain,
with derivative $(a,b,\nu)\mapsto(\rho a,b,\nu)$ at zero,
preserving ordinary base Coulomb gauge, the full zero-normal
condition, and exact attaching normalization. In these
coordinates the exact normalized equation is
\[
 \mathcal F(\Psi(U))=\mathcal A_{\rho,f}U+\mathcal N_{\rho,f}(U),
 \qquad \mathcal N_{\rho,f}(0)=D\mathcal N_{\rho,f}(0)=0.
\]
On a uniform ball in $X_{\rho,f}$ its remainder satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonlinear-cap-difference}
 \|\mathcal N_{\rho,f}(U)-\mathcal N_{\rho,f}(V)\|_Y
 \leq C(1+\rho M^2L^\varepsilon)
       (\|U\|_X+\|V\|_X)\|U-V\|_X,
\end{equation}
where $M=\|f\|_\infty$ and $L=1+\|d\log f\|_\infty$.
The constant is uniform for the caps in
\eqref{eq:capped-radius-range}, since
$\rho M^2L^\varepsilon\leq C\rho R^{4+\varepsilon}\to0$.
The coordinate assertion concerns the configuration space with
these gauge conditions, not the existence of a uniform slice
for physical gauge orbits.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We first give a surface construction at an irreducible flat
connection $\alpha$. Let $E_\alpha,H_\alpha,C_\alpha$ be its
Hodge projections. Choose a smooth flat Coulomb slice
$t\mapsto\alpha_t$ satisfying
\[
 [\alpha_t]=\operatorname{Exp}_{[\alpha]}t,\qquad
 E_\alpha(\alpha_t-\alpha)=0,\qquad
 D\alpha_t|_0=\ell t.
\]
For example, solve flatness of $\alpha+h+c$ for the coexact
component $c$, with small harmonic $h$. The derivative
$d_\alpha:C_\alpha H^k\to H^{k-1}(\Omega^2)$ is invertible
because $H^2_\alpha=H^0_\alpha=0$. The implicit function
theorem gives the slice, and a finite-dimensional change of
coordinates gives the stated exponential parameter.
For small $e\in E_\alpha H^k$ solve
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exact-coordinate-flat-chart}
 E_\alpha\bigl(\exp(\zeta)^*\alpha_t-\alpha\bigr)=e,
 \qquad \zeta\in H^{k+1}(\Omega^0).
\end{equation}
Use the gauge-action convention with derivative $d_\alpha\zeta$.
Its inverse is $G^0_\alpha d_\alpha^*$, so this is another
Banach implicit-function problem. Put
\[
 F_\alpha(e,t)=\exp(\zeta(e,t))^*\alpha_t,\qquad
 S_\alpha(e,t)=F_\alpha(e,t)-\alpha-e-\ell t.
\]
Then $S_\alpha$ is smooth and quadratic in $H^k$, and
$E_\alpha S_\alpha=0$. Flatness and Hodge decomposition give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:flat-chart-derivative-gain}
 \begin{aligned}
  d_\alpha S_\alpha&=-\tfrac12[q\wedge q],
       &q&=e+\ell t+S_\alpha,\\
  S_\alpha&=H_\alpha S_\alpha
              -\tfrac12d_\alpha^*G^2_\alpha[q\wedge q].
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Since $k>1$ on the surface, the wedge product belongs to $H^k$.
The last inverse has order minus one, and the harmonic term
has smooth finite-dimensional range. Thus $S_\alpha$ is a
smooth quadratic map into $H^{k+1}$, including its difference
and derivative estimates. Fixing the exact component in
\eqref{eq:exact-coordinate-flat-chart} is essential here.

Write $s=1+\varepsilon$, $\mathcal H_j=H_B^sH_\Sigma^j$,
and $\mathcal W_j=H_B^\varepsilon H_\Sigma^j$.
For $j\geq k$, the product estimates are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-chart-products}
 \mathcal H_j\mathcal H_j\subset\mathcal H_j,\qquad
 \mathcal H_j\mathcal W_j\subset\mathcal W_j.
\end{equation}
They follow from the surface Hilbert algebra and base dyadic
decomposition. For the second estimate, the low-frequency
strong factor is bounded in $L^\infty$. At frequency $2^n$,
the weak low-frequency sum is bounded in $L^\infty$ by
$C2^{(1-\varepsilon)n}\|v\|_{\mathcal W_j}$.
The weighted contribution of a strong block $u_n$ is therefore
$C2^{-\varepsilon n}(2^{sn}\|u_n\|_2)\|v\|_{\mathcal W_j}$.
The comparable-frequency term is bounded in $H^\varepsilon$
by $C2^{-\varepsilon n}(2^{sn}\|u_n\|_2)
(2^{\varepsilon n}\|v_n\|_2)$ and is summable.
These arguments apply to Hilbert algebra valued blocks, with
constants independent of surface spectral truncation.
For the underlying scalar paraproduct and finite-dimensional
composition estimates, see
\cite[\S\S3.1, 3.5, especially (3.1.20) and (3.5.1)--(3.5.13)]
{Taylor91}.

Pull the actual flat boundary family back to a fixed collar,
and denote this flat family by $\alpha_z$. Its needed fixed-order
derivatives are uniformly bounded by
Lemma~\ref{lem:cap-dirichlet-metric}.
Apply \eqref{eq:exact-coordinate-flat-chart} directly in the
mixed spaces: its derivative $d_{\alpha_z}$ has bounded inverse
$G^0_{\alpha_z}d_{\alpha_z}^*$ from
$E_{\alpha_z}\mathcal H_k$ to $\mathcal H_{k+1}$.
The gauge products are smooth by \eqref{eq:cap-chart-products}.
The slice depends on finitely many parameters, so its composition
with $H^s$ base sections is smooth. One may first use its analytic
harmonic coordinates and then the smooth finite-dimensional
exponential change. The implicit function theorem initially
gives $S_z$ in $\mathcal H_k$;
\eqref{eq:flat-chart-derivative-gain}, with the varying Hodge
operators, improves its range to $\mathcal H_{k+1}$.
For a fixed collar cutoff $\chi=1$ on the seam, set
\[
 S(b)(z)=\chi(z)S_{\alpha_z}
       \bigl(E_{\alpha_z}b,\ell_z^{-1}H_{\alpha_z}b\bigr),
\]
extended by zero outside the collar. We have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-chart-smoothing}
 \|S(b)-S(c)\|_{\mathcal H_{k+1}}
 \leq C(\|b\|_{\mathcal H_k}+\|c\|_{\mathcal H_k})
                         \|b-c\|_{\mathcal H_k}.
\end{equation}
This is proved in the mixed spaces themselves; it does not
require a composition theorem for arbitrary nonlinear maps
between infinite-dimensional Sobolev spaces.

Define the configuration chart by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonlinear-cap-coordinates}
 \Psi(a,b,\nu)=
 (A_B+\rho a,\ \beta+b+S(b),\ \operatorname{Exp}_{u_E}\nu).
\end{equation}
At the seam its surface connection is $F_\beta(e,t)+c$,
where $c=C_\beta b$ and $t=\ell^{-1}H_\beta b$.
If $c=0$ and $t=\nu$, it is flat with the correct class.
Conversely, for small $e,t$, the derivative
$d_{F_\beta(e,t)}:C_\beta H^k\to H^{k-1}(\Omega^2)$
remains invertible. Its unique small flat correction is $c=0$.
Equality of classes then implies $t=\nu$ in the exponential
coordinate ball. This proves both implications of nonlinear
matching. The boundary traces have order $1/2+\varepsilon$
with values in $H_\Sigma^k$ and are continuous; curvature
there is interpreted in $H_\Sigma^{k-1}$. No boundary trace
of the weak graph derivative is used.
At $z_0$ one has $\alpha_z=\alpha$ and $E_\alpha S(b)(z_0)=0$,
so the exact attaching normalization is unchanged. The base
conditions remain affine because the first component of
\eqref{eq:nonlinear-cap-coordinates} is $A_B+\rho a$.
This chart is not a four-dimensional gauge transformation.

The multiplier bound for $f/M$ and
\eqref{eq:cap-chart-smoothing} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-chart-graph-bound}
 \rho\|f d_\alpha(S(b)-S(c))\|_{\mathcal W_k}
 \leq C\rho ML^\varepsilon
       (\|b\|_{\mathcal H_k}+\|c\|_{\mathcal H_k})
                              \|b-c\|_{\mathcal H_k}.
\end{equation}
Here $\rho ML^\varepsilon\leq1$ for sufficiently small cap
scale. Thus $b\mapsto b+S(b)$ has a uniformly small Lipschitz
remainder on a uniform graph ball. Contraction gives its inverse
on a uniform smaller ball, and the Banach implicit function
theorem gives smoothness. Restricting this ambient chart by
the equivalences above gives precisely the stated linear domain.

It remains to estimate the equation. Put $B=b+S(b)$.
In the scalar sign convention already fixed, the negative
remainders are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cap-nonlinear-rows}
 \begin{aligned}
  \mathcal N_H
   &=-\frac{\rho}{2f}*_B[a\wedge a]
     -\rho f *_\Sigma d_\beta S(b)
     -\frac{\rho f}{2}*_\Sigma[B\wedge B],\\
  \mathcal N_V
   &=\bigl(d_BS(b)+[A_B,S(b)]+\rho[a,B]\bigr)^{0,2}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Indeed, the base curvature increment is
$\rho d_{A_B}a+\rho^2[a\wedge a]/2$ and its scalar
normalization divides by $\rho f$.
The three scalar terms have quadratic difference constants
$C\rho M^2L^\varepsilon$, $C\rho ML^\varepsilon$, and
$C\rho ML^\varepsilon$. For the middle one use the surface
derivative gained in \eqref{eq:cap-chart-smoothing}.
The mixed terms have constants $C,C,C\rho M$.
The norm controls $a/M,b,\nu$ in the strong space; base
differentiation of $S$ takes it to the weak space.
All background coefficients in these estimates are uniformly
bounded. On the positive side, smooth exponential coordinates
and a smooth target trivialization give a remainder consisting
of a coefficient vanishing at zero times $d_B\nu$, and a
zeroth-order quadratic term. Equation~\eqref{eq:cap-chart-products}
and finite-dimensional composition give its uniform difference
bound. Combining the estimates, and using $M,L\geq1$, proves
\eqref{eq:nonlinear-cap-difference}.
\end{proof}

This proposition concerns an exact cap. The complete preglued chart
and its estimates are proved in Proposition~\ref{prop:gluing-nonlinear-chart}.

\subsection{Localization and annular source errors}
\begin{lemma}[localization of the flat cap inverse]
\label{lem:flat-cap-localization}
Choose the fixed base metric flat in a half-disc coordinate
$\zeta=s+it$ at $z_0=0$. Use the cap $f=f_{R_1}$ of
\eqref{eq:euclidean-cap-factor}, with $r_1=R_1^{-1}$.
Let $Q_{\rho,f}$ be the flat nonharmonic inverse of
Lemma~\ref{lem:variable-factor-inverse}, with its domain
\eqref{eq:variable-factor-flat-norm}. Suppose the target
$F=(H,v)$ vanishes for $|\zeta|<r_0$, where
$r_1<c r_0$ for a fixed sufficiently small $c>0$ and $r_0$
is smaller than the fixed coordinate radius.
For $U=(a,x,y)=Q_{\rho,f}F$, write
$a=*_B d\psi$ with $\psi|_{\partial B}=0$.
Choose a radial cutoff $\chi$, zero for $|\zeta|\leq4r_1$
and one for $|\zeta|\geq8r_1$, with fixed rescaled profile.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stream-cutoff}
 \mathcal C_1U=\bigl(*_B d(\chi\psi),\chi x,\chi y\bigr)
\end{equation}
belongs to the same domain, vanishes near the changed cap metric,
and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:flat-cap-localization}
 \|\mathcal C_1U-U\|_{X_{\rho,f}}
 +\|\mathcal A_{\rho,f}\mathcal C_1U-F\|_Y
 \leq C r_0^{-6}r_1^{1-2\varepsilon}\|F\|_Y .
\end{equation}
The constant is independent of scale and every surface frequency.
The norms in this statement are the compact-base norms; a global
physical norm is not asserted.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Set $m=\rho\Lambda$, interpreted initially in a single surface
mode. The cap obeys
$M\asymp r_1^{-2}$, $L\leq Cr_1^{-1}$, and
$f(\zeta)\asymp(|\zeta|^2+r_1^2)^{-1}$ near zero.
The function $\widehat f(Z)=r_1^2f(r_1Z)$ has uniform
positive lower bound and fixed-order derivative bounds on
every fixed rescaled half-disc.
The base Hodge decomposition and
\eqref{eq:variable-factor-stream} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:localized-stream-equations}
 \begin{gathered}
  v=dx+*_Bdz,\qquad y=z+m\psi,\qquad z|_{\partial B}=0,\\
  (\Delta+m^2f^2)\psi=fH-mf^2z,\qquad
  (\Delta+m^2f^2)y=\Delta z+mfH.
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Here $x(z_0)=0$ is retained. The Hodge estimate bounds
$x,z$ in $H^{1+\varepsilon}$ by $C\|F\|_Y$; the flat
inverse bounds $y$ there by $CL^\varepsilon\|F\|_Y$.
Where $F=0$, $x,z$ are conjugate harmonic functions, with
Neumann and Dirichlet boundary conditions respectively,
and both vanish at zero. Their fixed-order rescaled norms
on $D_{64}^+$ are bounded by
$C_j(r_1/r_0)\|F\|_Y$. This follows from reflection and
harmonic derivative estimates, decreasing $c$ if necessary.

The norm of the homogeneous solution $y$ is subharmonic by
Kato's inequality. Comparison with the harmonic function
zero on the diameter and one on the semicircle gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:localized-coexact-vanishing}
 \|y(\zeta)\|_{H_\Sigma^k}
 \leq C\frac{\operatorname{Im}\zeta}{r_0}
                   L^\varepsilon\|F\|_Y,\qquad |\zeta|<r_0/2.
\end{equation}
To use derivatives uniformly in the mass, consider
$(-\Delta_{\rm Eucl}+n^2g^2)w=0$ on nested fixed half-discs,
with zero trace on the straight edge and $g$ uniformly
positive and smooth. Caccioppoli controls $dw$ and $nw$
on a smaller half-disc by the outer $L^2$ norm. Apply it
repeatedly to $n^jw$, which satisfies the same equation.
This controls every fixed power of $n$. Ordinary local
